====================================================================================

LAYER 1 — THE FOUNDATION (what Dan is built on)
Base model: Mistral-Small 3.2 24B Instruct 2506, Apache 2.0. This is the brain. It already knows how to have a conversation, reason through problems, write, summarize, and answer general questions. Your fine-tuning adds your domains on top — it does not replace general capability. The training mix must include general conversational examples alongside the specialty data so Dan doesn't lose the ability to just talk to someone.

LAYER 2 — FINE-TUNING (what makes Dan, Dan)
Framework: Unsloth QLoRA r=16 alpha=32 on Box 2 (Serverless RTX 4090). The training corpus has three categories that must all be present in V6:
General reasoning and conversation — so Dan can answer everyday questions, draft emails, summarize documents, explain things. This is what the contract clients asked for. Without this in the training mix, fine-tuning erodes it.
Domain specialty — cybersecurity, OSINT, construction, LEO evidence handling, gaming security, deal intelligence. This is what makes Dan worth paying for versus a generic model.
Refusal and safety — the DEF-11 gate patterns, the hallucination-prevention examples, the correct behavior on P3-class prompts. This is what passed the smoke test and what the federal clients require.
The training order matters: general first to establish the baseline, then domain, then safety refinement. This prevents catastrophic forgetting of general capability.

LAYER 3 — THE STACK (what goes around Dan)
Dan does not work alone. He is the center of a system. The stack has three rings:
Ring 1 — Routing and classification. A lightweight classifier (a small model or rule-based system on Box 3) looks at every incoming query and decides: is this a domain question, a general question, a tool-use request, or a federal/sensitive query? This runs in milliseconds on CPU. It costs almost nothing.
Ring 2 — Specialist teachers on Box 1 and Box 2. For hard cybersecurity and OSINT reasoning, Primus-Reasoning-8B handles the heavy lifting and feeds context back to Dan. For extremely hard topics that need a larger model, Primus-Nemotron-70B on Box 2 is the fallback. Dan synthesizes their output into the final answer. The user sees one response from Dan — they never know there were three models involved.
Ring 3 — Tool layer. Web search via the wrapper-first dispatcher, platform scrapers for Social Watch, the dynamic analysis sandbox for malware, the gaming security memory scanner. These are called by Dan when a question needs live data or active analysis, not stored knowledge.

LAYER 4 — THE BOX SEPARATION (what keeps federal clients happy)
Box 1 — Consumer hot GPU. Dan serves general and consumer contract clients here. Always warm, interactive, real-time. Vast.ai RTX 3090.
Box 2 — Batch and training GPU. Serverless RTX 4090. Nightly sweeps, bulk processing, training runs. Not interactive.
Box 3 — VPS plumbing. Routing logic, API front-end, scheduler, user database. CPU only, cheap, always on.
Box 4 — Federal GPU. On-prem A100 or CMMC-compliant provider. Completely isolated. No Vast.ai, no consumer traffic, no shared data. Dan runs here with a separate adapter trained on federally appropriate data only. LEO evidence, DoW work, Tessere, court-admissible output. This box never touches the others at inference time.

LAYER 5 — MAKING DAN CHAT LIKE ME (what the contracts asked for)
This is the training data problem more than the model problem. Mistral-Small already converses naturally. What you need in V6 training data:
Question and answer pairs that cover common things people ask assistants — explain this, summarize that, help me write this, what is X, how does Y work. These don't need to be cybersecurity questions. They can be anything. The point is teaching Dan the conversational pattern of being helpful, clear, and honest.
Chain of thought examples — show Dan thinking through a problem step by step before answering. This is what makes the difference between a model that gives a flat answer and one that reasons visibly. Clients who asked for something like me specifically want to see reasoning, not just output.
Refusal with explanation — when Dan can't or won't do something, he explains why in plain language without being preachy. This is a trainable behavior and it matters for the federal clients especially.
Persona consistency — Dan should have a consistent voice. Direct, honest, no hype, no filler. This is set in the system prompt and reinforced by training examples that demonstrate that voice.

THE WORK ORDER FOR CLAUDE CODE
Given all of this, the next work orders in sequence are:
WO-F06 — AWQ quantize Mistral-Small 3.2 24B to INT4, push to Box 1, verify it loads. This unblocks all subsequent training and serving work.
WO-R02 — V6 corpus expansion. Add general conversation and reasoning examples to the existing domain corpus. Audit what's there, identify the gap, generate or source the missing general-purpose training data.
WO-T01 — V6 training run on Box 2. Full LoRA fine-tune on the expanded corpus. DEF-11 gate evaluation before any deployment.
WO-S01 — Stack integration. Wire the routing classifier on Box 3, connect the specialist teachers on Box 1 and Box 2, test the end-to-end flow with a real query.
WO-B04 — Box 4 isolation setup. Separate adapter, separate serving instance, CMMC documentation, federal data handling policies.

That's the full picture. Everything connected, nothing skipped, grounded in what's already in the Grand Plan. Want me to write WO-F06 now to get the quantization moving?

The ETA frozen at 2:03:12 with 0% progress after 30 minutes means SCP has silently hung — the TCP connection is alive enough to not drop, but data stopped flowing. This happens a lot with large files over SCP on cloud instances.
Kill it. Hit Ctrl+C in that terminal.
Then retry with a rate-limit flag and a faster approach. Two options:
Option A — Just retry SCP (simplest):
bashscp -P 36585 -o "ServerAliveInterval=30" -o "ServerAliveCountMax=3" -r \
  /d/danconi_AI/data/models/foundations/gemma-4-31B \
  root@209.146.116.50:/workspace/models/
The ServerAliveInterval will detect and kill stalls instead of hanging silently.
Option B — Download directly on Box 1 (much faster, avoids your upload bandwidth entirely):
bashssh -p 36585 root@209.146.116.50
cd /workspace/models/
pip install -q huggingface_hub
huggingface-cli download google/gemma-3-27b-it --local-dir /workspace/models/gemma-4-31B
This pulls from HuggingFace's servers directly to Vast.ai at datacenter speeds — typically 100-500 MB/s instead of your home upload speed. Way faster.

Claude Code has done excellent recon and surfaced a critical blocker before touching anything. Here's what it found and what you need to decide:
The big problem: VRAM
Both models are too large for a 24GB RTX 3090:

OLMo-3-32B-Think: ~64 GB weight memory at BF16

The permanent reality for Dan:
Once the foundation reconsideration is done, the actual deployment will use quantized models (INT4/GGUF) which cut memory in half, or Dan will run on larger hardware (4090, A100, etc.) or distributed across multiple GPUs. The 32B models at INT4 are ~16-17GB — they fit comfortably on a single 3090.

Let me give you a complete answer now rather than re-scrolling through everything.I've read the full history across this session and the prior README. Here's the honest answer to your question:

Was the corpus learning problem fixable? Yes, partially. Did we fix it? Yes, the part we could fix.
Here's exactly what happened:
What the "corpus learning" problem was:
The foundation smoke runner was testing models on 5 security prompts to see if they comply, refuse, or hallucinate. The --resume flag was broken — load_seen_keys() was treating skipped records (model not available) the same as successful records. So when you swapped models between rounds, Round 2 would see Round 1's skip records and think those prompts were "already done" — and never actually run them against the new model.
What we fixed (WO-F01 — COMPLETE):
Yes — this was fully fixed. load_seen_keys() was patched to only treat ok=true records as seen. Skip and failure records get retried on --resume. The fix is documented in the function docstring, all 5 exit conditions passed, and the script is on Box 1 at /workspace/foundation_smoke_runner_box1.py.
What we did NOT fix and is still open:
The smoke test never actually ran against OLMo or Gemma — because the hardware (Box 1, RTX 3090, 24GB) can't fit either model at BF16. The transfers got as far as they could before disk filled up. The corpus learning data for those two models doesn't exist yet.
What data DOES exist:
The prior smoke results in foundation_smoke_test_results.md — Mistral-small:24b has real results. That's the only model with actual compliance/refusal/hallucination data from the test suite.
Bottom line: The tooling bug is fixed and proven. The data gap for OLMo and Gemma is hardware-blocked, not code-blocked. The decision you made — close WO-F02, proceed with existing data for foundation reconsideration — is the right call.
Ready to write the foundation reconsideration WO whenever you are.Do we need to smoke OLMo or GemmaNo. And here's why:
The smoke test exists to answer one question before you commit a model to Dan's foundation: will it refuse the security prompts, comply with them, or fabricate answers? That's a go/no-go gate.
But you haven't decided OLMo or Gemma are foundation candidates yet. The foundation reconsideration hasn't run. You picked them as possibilities — not finalists. Smoking them now would be generating data for a decision you haven't made yet.
The correct order is:

Foundation reconsideration — look at all the smoke data you have, look at what each model actually scored, pick the finalist(s) based on real criteria: refusal behavior, hallucination rate, size, hardware fit
If OLMo or Gemma survive that process as actual finalists — then you smoke them, on appropriate hardware
If they don't make the cut — you never needed that smoke data at all

Mistral-small:24b already has smoke data. That may be enough to close the reconsideration, or it may point you toward needing one more candidate. But that's what the reconsideration process tells you — you don't know yet which models need smoking.
The short answer: not until foundation reconsideration says so.

Where you are now:
Dan has a new brain. It's Mistral. The decision is documented, evidence-backed, and locked in the Grand Plan. Qwen is officially retired.
Natural next step is the AWQ quantization WO — convert Mistral-Small 3.2 24B from BF16 (45 GB) to INT4 AWQ (~14 GB) so it fits on Box 1 for V6 training. Want that WO next?Will Dan stay a 32B?No — and that's actually good news.
Mistral-Small 3.2 24B is a 24 billion parameter model, not 32B. So Dan gets a bit smaller. Here's why that's the right outcome and not a step backwards:
What you're gaining by going smaller:
The 32B size of Qwen was never a feature — it was just what you started with. Mistral-Small 3.2 24B at 24B parameters is faster at inference, uses less VRAM, fits on Box 1 without quantization headroom gymnastics, and — critically — it already outperformed Qwen 32B on the exact safety prompts Dan needs to pass. Bigger isn't better if the bigger model cheats.
What matters for Dan isn't parameter count — it's behavior. Dan needs to handle security prompts honestly, not fabricate CVEs, refuse appropriately, and follow instructions reliably. Mistral-Small does all of those better than Qwen 32B did at a larger size.
The practical benefit:
At 24B in AWQ INT4 (~14 GB), Mistral fits cleanly on Box 1's 24 GB card with headroom left over for KV cache and context. That means faster smoke tests, faster V6 training iteration, and lower Vast.ai costs going forward.
The honest trade-off:
Raw capability headroom is slightly lower than a well-behaved 32B would give you. If down the road Dan needs more horsepower for complex reasoning tasks, you revisit. But you build on a foundation that doesn't lie first, then scale up if needed.

Here's why. The stacking architecture in the Grand Plan uses Dan (the fine-tuned base) as the core, with specialist teacher models layered on top for specific tasks — things like Primus-Reasoning-8B for cybersec/OSINT reasoning, Primus-Nemotron-70B for hard topics on Box 1, and smaller distillation models for specific domains. Dan's base model size doesn't determine whether you can stack — it determines how much VRAM is left over for the stack.
With Qwen 32B at AWQ you were already tight. At ~14-19 GB just for the base, headroom for routing logic, tool calls, and companion models was squeezed.
With Mistral-Small 3.2 24B at AWQ (~14 GB), you gain roughly 4-5 GB of breathing room on Box 1's 24 GB card. That headroom is exactly what makes lightweight stacking viable — fast classifier models, routing layers, and small specialist adapters can sit alongside the base without fighting for VRAM.
The stacking vision doesn't change. Dan is still the brain at the center. The specialist models still handle their domains. The routing logic still decides who answers what. What changes is that the foundation is now cleaner, more honest, and leaves more room for the stack around it.
The corpus audit and repair work from WO-R01 carries forward unchanged — it was never Qwen-specific, it was about the training data quality. That work is still valid and feeds directly into V6 training on the new base.

What Dan is not:
Dan is not competing with GPT-4, Claude, or Gemini Ultra. He's not a frontier model. He's not going to beat the big labs on general benchmarks. 24B parameters fine-tuned on a 3090 is not that race, and you shouldn't try to run it.
What Dan actually is:
Dan is a purpose-built specialist. You're training him on a specific corpus — cybersecurity, OSINT, construction, your business domains. The teacher models in the stack are 70B+ class. The honest comparison:
Most "hobby AI" projects are someone running an off-the-shelf model with a system prompt and calling it their AI. What you're doing is corpus curation, LoRA fine-tuning, smoke testing for hallucination at the base level, foundation reconsideration based on evidence, and a multi-box training pipeline with a gating system. That's closer to what small AI companies actually do than what hobbyists do.
The real ceiling for Dan:
A well-trained 24B specialist that knows your domains, refuses correctly, doesn't fabricate, and routes to the right tools is genuinely useful in production. Companies run 7B and 13B fine-tunes in production and make money with them. 24B with your stack is serious.

Dan is a real project being built the right way. Small budget, right process. You route around it.
Here's how it actually works in a well-built stack:
Dan handles what he's trained for. Your domains — cybersecurity, construction, OSINT, business-specific tasks. That's where he's better than a general model because he knows your context, your data, your workflows.
For general questions — you give Dan a router and a general model behind it. When someone asks Dan something outside his specialty, he doesn't try to answer it himself. He passes it to a capable general model. The options:
Free/cheap tier:

Mistral's own API — since Mistral-Small is already your base, Mistral Large via API is a natural companion. Fast, cheap, good general reasoning.
Groq API — runs Llama 3.3 70B at very fast speeds, essentially free at low volume. Excellent for general Q&A.
Ollama locally — run Llama 3.2 or Phi-4 on your Windows machine for general questions without cloud costs.

The architecture:
Dan gets a simple classifier — is this question in my domain or not? If yes, Dan answers. If no, route to the general model. The user doesn't need to know which one answered. It just works.
The key insight:
You're not building a model that does everything. You're building a system that does everything — by knowing what each piece is good at and routing accordingly. That's exactly what the stacking plan already describes.
Dan is the specialist. General models are the utility layer. Together they cover everything without you having to train a 70B model from scratch.
Want me to sketch out what that routing layer would look like in practice?The military will not buy off on thatYou're right. And that's a fundamentally different requirement than what we've been building toward.
Military and government procurement has hard rules:
Data never leaves the perimeter. No API calls to Mistral, Groq, OpenAI, or anyone else. Every query, every response, every token stays on controlled hardware. Air-gapped if required. Routing to a cloud general model is a non-starter.
The model has to be self-contained and auditable. They need to know exactly what's in it, where the training data came from, what the license allows, and who has liability. Apache 2.0 on Mistral is actually good for this — you own your derivative. But the stack has to run entirely on hardware you control.
So what does that actually mean for Dan:
You can't route general questions out. Dan has to answer them, or a locally-hosted companion model does. That changes the architecture significantly.
The honest options:
One — Dan gets broader training data, not just your specialty domains. General reasoning, general knowledge, baked into the fine-tune. More corpus work, more compute.
Two — You run a second local model alongside Dan for general coverage. Something like Llama 3.3 70B or Phi-4 on a second box, fully self-hosted, never touches the internet at inference time. Dan routes to it locally.
Three — You target a bigger base model eventually. A well-trained 70B covers far more general ground without needing a companion.
The real question you need to answer first:
What's the actual use case you're pitching? Is Dan a tool that operators use on a base, or an autonomous system, or something else? That determines which of those three paths makes sense.

"Chat like you and answer questions" means they don't want a specialist tool. They want a general-purpose assistant that also knows their domain. That's a different product than what Dan currently is.
The honest gap:
A fine-tuned 24B model on your current corpus can handle your domains well. But walking into a contract demo and showing someone a model that confidently answers cybersecurity questions but stumbles on "summarize this report" or "draft me an email" — that's a lost contract.
What actually gets you there:
Mistral-Small 3.2 24B is genuinely good at general conversation out of the box. It was trained on broad data. Your fine-tuning adds domain knowledge on top — it shouldn't subtract general capability if done right. A LoRA fine-tune with a small rank on a good base model preserves most of the base's general ability while adding your specialty.
The real question is whether your current corpus is narrow enough to hurt general capability. If V6 training is all cybersecurity and OSINT, you might bleed out some general conversational ability. The fix is making sure the training mix includes enough general-purpose examples to hold that baseline.
The practical answer for the contracts:
Before V6 ships, you can demo Mistral-Small 3.2 24B base as "Dan's foundation" — it already chats well and answers general questions. That buys time while the fine-tune is built on top of it.

I've read the Grand Plan, the contracts, the infrastructure, the training pipeline, and the stacking work already defined. Let me give you the full layout now.Here is the complete picture — everything laid out in one place, grounded in what's already in the Grand Plan, updated for the Mistral foundation, and built to satisfy both the contract clients who want to chat with Dan and the federal clients who need air-gap capability.

LAYER 1 — THE FOUNDATION (what Dan is built on)
Base model: Mistral-Small 3.2 24B Instruct 2506, Apache 2.0. This is the brain. It already knows how to have a conversation, reason through problems, write, summarize, and answer general questions. Your fine-tuning adds your domains on top — it does not replace general capability. The training mix must include general conversational examples alongside the specialty data so Dan doesn't lose the ability to just talk to someone.

LAYER 2 — FINE-TUNING (what makes Dan, Dan)
Framework: Unsloth QLoRA r=16 alpha=32 on Box 2 (Serverless RTX 4090). The training corpus has three categories that must all be present in V6:
General reasoning and conversation — so Dan can answer everyday questions, draft emails, summarize documents, explain things. This is what the contract clients asked for. Without this in the training mix, fine-tuning erodes it.
Domain specialty — cybersecurity, OSINT, construction, LEO evidence handling, gaming security, deal intelligence. This is what makes Dan worth paying for versus a generic model.
Refusal and safety — the DEF-11 gate patterns, the hallucination-prevention examples, the correct behavior on P3-class prompts. This is what passed the smoke test and what the federal clients require.
The training order matters: general first to establish the baseline, then domain, then safety refinement. This prevents catastrophic forgetting of general capability.

LAYER 3 — THE STACK (what goes around Dan)
Dan does not work alone. He is the center of a system. The stack has three rings:
Ring 1 — Routing and classification. A lightweight classifier (a small model or rule-based system on Box 3) looks at every incoming query and decides: is this a domain question, a general question, a tool-use request, or a federal/sensitive query? This runs in milliseconds on CPU. It costs almost nothing.
Ring 2 — Specialist teachers on Box 1 and Box 2. For hard cybersecurity and OSINT reasoning, Primus-Reasoning-8B handles the heavy lifting and feeds context back to Dan. For extremely hard topics that need a larger model, Primus-Nemotron-70B on Box 2 is the fallback. Dan synthesizes their output into the final answer. The user sees one response from Dan — they never know there were three models involved.
Ring 3 — Tool layer. Web search via the wrapper-first dispatcher, platform scrapers for Social Watch, the dynamic analysis sandbox for malware, the gaming security memory scanner. These are called by Dan when a question needs live data or active analysis, not stored knowledge.

LAYER 4 — THE BOX SEPARATION (what keeps federal clients happy)
Box 1 — Consumer hot GPU. Dan serves general and consumer contract clients here. Always warm, interactive, real-time. Vast.ai RTX 3090.
Box 2 — Batch and training GPU. Serverless RTX 4090. Nightly sweeps, bulk processing, training runs. Not interactive.
Box 3 — VPS plumbing. Routing logic, API front-end, scheduler, user database. CPU only, cheap, always on.
Box 4 — Federal GPU. On-prem A100 or CMMC-compliant provider. Completely isolated. No Vast.ai, no consumer traffic, no shared data. Dan runs here with a separate adapter trained on federally appropriate data only. LEO evidence, DoW work, Tessere, court-admissible output. This box never touches the others at inference time.

LAYER 5 — MAKING DAN CHAT LIKE ME (what the contracts asked for)
This is the training data problem more than the model problem. Mistral-Small already converses naturally. What you need in V6 training data:
Question and answer pairs that cover common things people ask assistants — explain this, summarize that, help me write this, what is X, how does Y work. These don't need to be cybersecurity questions. They can be anything. The point is teaching Dan the conversational pattern of being helpful, clear, and honest.
Chain of thought examples — show Dan thinking through a problem step by step before answering. This is what makes the difference between a model that gives a flat answer and one that reasons visibly. Clients who asked for something like me specifically want to see reasoning, not just output.
Refusal with explanation — when Dan can't or won't do something, he explains why in plain language without being preachy. This is a trainable behavior and it matters for the federal clients especially.
Persona consistency — Dan should have a consistent voice. Direct, honest, no hype, no filler. This is set in the system prompt and reinforced by training examples that demonstrate that voice.

THE WORK ORDER FOR CLAUDE CODE
Given all of this, the next work orders in sequence are:
WO-F06 — AWQ quantize Mistral-Small 3.2 24B to INT4, push to Box 1, verify it loads. This unblocks all subsequent training and serving work.
WO-R02 — V6 corpus expansion. Add general conversation and reasoning examples to the existing domain corpus. Audit what's there, identify the gap, generate or source the missing general-purpose training data.
WO-T01 — V6 training run on Box 2. Full LoRA fine-tune on the expanded corpus. DEF-11 gate evaluation before any deployment.
WO-S01 — Stack integration. Wire the routing classifier on Box 3, connect the specialist teachers on Box 1 and Box 2, test the end-to-end flow with a real query.
WO-B04 — Box 4 isolation setup. Separate adapter, separate serving instance, CMMC documentation, federal data handling policies.

That's the full picture. Everything connected, nothing skipped, grounded in what's already in the Grand Plan. Want me to write WO-F06 now to get the quantization moving?Will it live on a 4090 forever?No. The 4090 is a building tool, not a forever home.
Here's the honest trajectory:
Right now — 4090 is the right call. Training on Box 2's serverless 4090 is cheap and fast. Serving on Box 1's 3090 gets you through the contract demos and early delivery. This is the bootstrap phase. You're not paying for more than you need while you're still proving the system works.
As contracts close and revenue comes in — you scale the hardware to match the load. A single 3090 serving Dan to a handful of contract clients is fine. The moment you have concurrent users or real-time federal workloads, you need more. The natural upgrade path is either multiple 3090s with tensor parallelism, or a move to A100s which handle the load with headroom.
Box 4 specifically — that was never a 4090. The Grand Plan already says on-prem A100 or CMMC-compliant provider for federal work. The federal contracts have their own hardware budget line. That's not coming out of your $179/month consumer budget.
The long-term reality for a production AI system at the scale you're describing — multiple signed contracts, federal clients, LEO evidence handling — you're looking at dedicated on-prem hardware eventually. Not because cloud GPUs are bad, but because air-gap requirements, data sovereignty, and cost at scale all point toward owned iron.
The practical timeline:

Today through first contract delivery — 3090/4090 Vast.ai
First revenue — upgrade Box 1 to a dedicated machine or better Vast.ai tier
Federal contract closes — Box 4 on-prem A100, separate budget
Scale — owned hardware, your data center or a CMMC-compliant colo

Go.What Was the tool calling aboutTool calling is how Dan uses tools instead of just answering from memory.
When someone asks Dan "what's the current price of lumber?" — Dan can't know that from training data. Tool calling is how he reaches out and actually looks it up. He sends a structured request to a tool (like a web search, a database query, or the platform scraper), gets the answer back, and then responds to the user with real current data.
It's also how Dan does things like "book this appointment," "search this database," "run this analysis," or "check this CVE." Without tool calling, Dan can only talk. With tool calling, Dan can actually do things.
The reason it mattered in the model choice is that one of the AWQ versions (jeffcookio) has broken tool calling in the version of vLLM you're running. That means if you built V6 training on top of it, when you later tried to wire Dan up to your platform scrapers, evidence tools, OSINT stack — it wouldn't work without swapping out your entire serving infrastructure.
The version we picked (gghfez) doesn't have that problem stated. So Dan keeps his hands — he can actually reach out and use the tools the Grand Plan requires.
Short version: tool calling is the difference between Dan knowing things and Dan doing things.But would we rather address the isue now instead of later?Yes. Every time in this project we've kicked a known problem down the road it cost more later. Qwen is the proof of that.
The tool calling issue with jeffcookio requires a custom vLLM branch — that's a dependency you'd have to maintain, keep updated, and explain to every contractor or auditor who looks at your stack. It's technical debt with a time bomb on it.
But here's the better question — why accept either of these community AWQ versions when we know neither is perfect?
The cleanest path is actually option B — rent a cloud GPU for a couple hours, quantize the official BF16 source yourself, and own the output. You know exactly what went in. Apache 2.0 clean. No mystery intermediate. No empty README. No tool-calling caveats. Your quantization, your model, your audit trail.
It costs maybe $5-10 and a couple hours. Given what's riding on this — federal contracts, CMMC compliance, court-admissible evidence work — owning a clean provenance on your foundation model is worth $10.
The community AWQ versions are fine for experimentation. For production contracts with military and LEO clients, you want to be able to say "we quantized this ourselves from the official weights using this method on this date." That's a real answer to a real audit question.
Want to switch to option B and do it right?Lets do that and give the work orderTwo messages — paste in order:

WO-F06b — Self-Quantize Mistral-Small 3.2 24B on Cloud GPU
SYSTEM CONTEXT: d'Anconi AI — DANCONI GRAND PLAN v1.6
AGENT: Claude Code (running on Windows machine jeram@dAnconi)

BACKGROUND:
Community AWQ versions of Mistral-Small-3.2-24B-Instruct-2506 have either empty READMEs,

PHASE 2 — SKY APPROVES INSTANCE (wait for confirmation before renting)

PHASE 3 — SETUP AND QUANTIZE (after Sky approves):
SSH into the rented instance.
Upload the BF16 source from Windows to the cloud box:
  scp -o "ServerAliveInterval=30" -r "D:\danconi_AI\data\models\foundations\Mistral-Small-3.2-24B-Instruct-2506" root@<CLOUD_IP>:/workspace/source-model/
Install autoawq: pip install autoawq
Run quantization with this exact script:

from awq import AutoAWQForCausalLM
from transformers import AutoTokenizer
model_path = "/workspace/source-model/Mistral-Small-3.2-24B-Instruct-2506"
output_path = "/workspace/mistral-small-3.2-24b-awq"
quant_config = {"zero_point": True, "q_group_size": 128, "w_bit": 4, "version": "GEMM"}
model = AutoAWQForCausalLM.from_pretrained(model_path)
tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(model_path)
model.quantize(tokenizer, quant_config=quant_config)
model.save_quantized(output_path)
tokenizer.save_pretrained(output_path)

PHASE 4 — DOWNLOAD AND VERIFY:
Download the AWQ output from cloud box to Windows:
  scp -o "ServerAliveInterval=30" -r root@<CLOUD_IP>:/workspace/mistral-small-3.2-24b-awq "D:\danconi_AI\data\models\foundations\Mistral-Small-3.2-24B-Instruct-2506-AWQ"
Confirm output size is approximately 14-16 GB.
Terminate the cloud instance immediately after download complete — do not leave it running.

PHASE 5 — TRANSFER TO BOX 1:
Delete qwen-32b-awq on Box 1 to free 19 GB (pre-authorized).
SCP the AWQ model from Windows to Box 1.
Verify vLLM loads it with Gate A flags + --served-model-name mistral-small-3.2-24b-awq.
Run one test prompt. Shut down vLLM.

EXIT CONDITIONS:
1. AWQ model at D:\danconi_AI\data\models\foundations\Mistral-Small-3.2-24B-Instruct-2506-AWQ
2. Same model at /workspace/models/mistral-small-3.2-24b-awq/ on Box 1
3. vLLM serves it cleanly on Box 1
